# Existing optimisations the new UI must not undo

Tracker row N17, written 2026-11-16. Read-only inventory: no product code changed. **AGENTS rule 14:** before you replace or rewrite a table, log viewer, terminal, editor, data hook or startup path, read the section for it here and keep or re-express every item on its "must not regress" list. If what you touch is not covered, stop and add it here first.

**Status of this file.** Complete as of 2026-11-16: §1 (tables, the part slice step 1a.3 waits on), §2 to §10 (the registers), §11 (the master must-not-regress list) and §12 (what I could not assess), then Appendix A (work that is not on `main`) and Appendix B (the pull requests cited). Every `file:line` citation (1,584 of them) was resolved by script against its pinned tree before this was committed: the file exists, the line is in range and not blank. That proves the location exists; it does not prove each claim, which the rows state with their source.

| Shorthand | Expands to |

**Verdicts.**

- **CARRY AS ENGINE**: the code is UI-independent and worth keeping as it is. It moves (copied with its provenance noted, or stays where it is) behind a new API; the API is proposed in one to three lines.
- **RE-EXPRESS**: the technique must exist in the new tree, but the code is bound to the old markup, library or CSS, so it is written again. The row states the rule the new code must satisfy and the number or count that shows it does.
- **OBSOLETE**: the new tree does not need it; the row says why.

**Evidence.** Numbers are quoted from the source named next to them (a pull request description or comment, a benchmark README, a doc), never produced here: I ran no benchmark for this inventory. "Unmeasured" means no source reports a number. `[INFERENCE]` marks my own reasoning. Almost all recorded numbers come from a fixture backend inside the page, Chromium and an M5 Max; the only WKWebView numbers are the two the pull requests say were taken in the native app (host #487, Kubernetes #257). Read each PR's own "not verified" list before treating a number as a budget.

**Guards.** A guard is something that fails when the optimisation is undone. `G-existing` names one that runs today; `G-new` is a proposal with the exact assertion. A guard that lives in the frozen `lib` repository, or in a plugin's own UI tests, stops protecting the new tree at the flip, so it is marked "port": the test is to be rewritten against the new code, not trusted.

**Contents.** §1 Tables · §2 Resource data layer · §3 Log viewer · §4 Terminal and bottom drawer · §5 Editors · §6 Startup, plugin loading and build · §7 Tabs, panes and windows · §8 Events, subscriptions and idle timers · §9 First-party plugins · §10 Guards that exist and what they cannot see · §11 The MUST NOT REGRESS list · §12 What I could not assess.

**Headline findings, for the lead**

1. **Plan 3.3 item 2 picks spacer rows; the previous sprint measured "fixed rows in normal flow" as the worse layout** (live-update p95 +22.5%, layout +20.9%, reverted). Measure both layouts under live updates before choosing (T-03, 1.3).
2. **Three pieces are UI-independent and worth carrying as engines:** the adaptive sorted row model (-90.9% comparator calls, T-15), the virtualizer snapshot adapter (T-05), the age clock (3,000 timers to 1, T-26).
3. **The plan's decision rule B1 to B7 has five gaps** (1.4): no live-update workload, no work-count guards, no WKWebView check for "native black rows", no metric-state matrix, no memory check. B2 (16.7 ms p95) is also stricter than anything recorded for the old table (33.4 ms unthrottled, 66.7 ms with sparse updates).
4. **No benchmark can fail a pull request and none mounts the new tree** (§10). CI runs the render bench and the whole-app profile as report-only jobs on the old shell; two benchmark scripts target a file that was deleted (`useEventBatcher.ts`).
5. **A bounded first set (A10) cannot come from a source view:** the host emits no rows before `snapshotEnd`, in Go map order, in frames of at most 16 records, while the benchmark fixture emits 256 (§2, MNR-D3, MNR-D4).
6. **There is no lazy Monaco today:** it is 22.8% of the minified entry's characters, and two whole icon sets are 39.1% (§5, §6).
7. **Tabs shown once stay mounted hidden with no cap, and almost none of the keep-alive policy is tested** (§7).
8. **The ui-next skeleton's bare `new QueryClient()` lacks the old shell's `refetchOnWindowFocus: false`** (RTM-14).
9. **Defects not to carry:** an unbounded log pause buffer and terminal listeners never removed on teardown (3.1, 4.1).
10. **Documentation drift:** the Kubernetes README still says the table "keeps at least 32 rows" of overscan; the code uses the library default 10 since K8s #241 (T-01).

## 1. Tables

### 1.1 Where the cost of a table is controlled today

The Pods table is the plugin's own table, not the host's generic one. Both sit on the same library table and the same runtime hooks. A change in the new tree can undo an optimisation at any of five layers; the register in 1.2 is ordered by layer.

| Layer | Code | What it owns |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Delivery and acquisition | runtime `useResources`, source views, `sharePage` (`RT/hooks/resource/useResources.ts`, `RT/resource/v2/sourceView.ts`) | how rows reach the page, how often, and which records keep their object identity (§2 has the full register; 1.2 lists what the table depends on) |
| 2. Row model | plugin `decodeResourceTableRows`, `decodeRecordBody` (`KT/ResourceTable/rows.ts`, `k8s:ui/src/utils/recordBody.ts`); host `projectResourceRows` (`HT/rows.ts`) | one decoded body per `HostRecord` object; the reverse lookup that actions and metric demand use |
| 3. Table state | TanStack Table through `useTable` / `useDataTable`, the feature set, the adaptive sorted row model (`DT/constants.ts`, `DT/hooks/useDataTable.ts`, `DT/utils/createAdaptiveSortedRowModel.ts`) | sort, filter, selection, visibility, pinning, sizing |
| 4. Window and rows | `DataTable.VirtualBody` (`DT/DataTableVirtualBody.tsx`), `useVirtualizerSnapshot` (`lib:packages/base-ui/src/system/useVirtualizerSnapshot.ts`), `DT/DataTable.module.css` | which rows exist as DOM, how they are positioned, which re-render |
| 5. Cells and shared owners | `KC/cells/*`, `KC/TableMetrics.tsx`, `KT/TableActionsScope.tsx`, shared menu and tooltip handles | per-cell timers, popups, listeners, metric demand |

Rows are named `T-nn`. "MNR" in the verdict column means the item is on the must-not-regress list in 1.5. "Slice" names the plan step that touches it (`docs/ui-next/slices/1a-pods-list.md`, section 4.1). Quoted counts are exact; "(host #470 comment)" is the comment thread of that pull request, "recorded evidence" is `bench/app/renderer-sprint-metal-evidence-2026-11-10.json`.

**Layer 3: table state**

### 1.3 Measured and rejected (do not repeat without new numbers)

These are the experiments the previous sprint tried and reverted or rejected, from the host #470 comment ("Rejected, not shipped") and `bench/app/README.md:835-841`. Each ran on a fixture backend, Chromium on Metal, M5 Max, two samples per side per workload ("screening only, not acceptance", host #470 comment). They are warnings, not laws: the new table's own measurement overrules them. A variant that matches one of them must beat the recorded number under the live-update workload (G-T1, 1.4), not only under plain scrolling.

| Experiment | Result in its matched controls | Decision then | Relevance to slice 1a |
|---|---|---|---|
| Larger retained window than 32 rows per side | no-update p99 +94.5% | reverted. (The 24 and 32 row buffers themselves shipped in K8s #228 and #230 and were removed in K8s #241, "Use the shared table's overscan policy instead of overriding it in the plugin", commit 19c2195: the library default 10 with 4-row steps applies today) | plan 3.3 item 3: overscan six, measured not assumed |
| Two-row range batches | no-update p99 +16.7%, JavaScript +16.5% | reverted | T-02 |
| Eight-row range batches | no-update p95 +11.2% | reverted | T-02 |
| Compositor layer per row | p99 +11.9% | rejected | no `will-change` or `translateZ` on rows |
| Memoise scalar metric cells | live-update p95 +11.9%, p99 +16.2% | reverted | T-06, T-27: memoise rows and the expensive metric view, not each scalar |
| **Fixed rows in normal flow (spacers)** | live-update p95 +22.5%, layout +20.9% | reverted | **plan 3.3 item 2 chooses spacer rows**: measure both (T-03) |
| Non-inheriting row-position properties | style work +17.8%, p95 -1.0% | rejected | T-03 |
| Recycle row and cell DOM shells | no-update p95 +8.2% at 2x CPU slowdown | reverted | no DOM-recycling layer for rows |
| Costs the retained set did NOT improve | first row +2.0%, DOM elements at rest +2.8%, healthy metric target slots +21.1%, observation opens 0% | recorded | a bigger window costs demand and DOM; "no startup speedup claimed" |

### 1.4 What the plan's decision rule does not cover

Plan 3.3 sets B1 to B7. Measured against what the old table's optimisations actually protect, five things are missing. Each is a proposed guard; none exists yet.

1. **G-T1: a sparse live-update workload.** Every retained win of the previous sprint showed up under updates (the adaptive sort, memoisation, range reuse), and plain scrolling hides them: the old team's no-update frame p95 did not improve (116.6 to 150.1 ms at 4x CPU) while the sparse-update run improved (1,199.9 to 250 ms, both "still unacceptable"; `bench/app/README.md:863-864`). B2 scrolls a static list. Add a B8 beside it: the same scripted scroll with the seeded sparse-update workload already in the repo (`bench/render/fixtures/updates.ts:133`, `--updates sparse`: 5 events a second touching 2 Pods, every tenth touch a delete and recreate), on 5,000 Pods, at 1x and 4x CPU throttle, comparing rAF p95, driver lateness p95 and comparator calls. Run it on both layout variants of T-03.
2. **G-T2: work counts, not times.** Times drift between passes on this machine (a startup median moved up to 16%, one scroll metric 46%: `docs/ui-next/baseline/2026-11-16.md:56-62`); counts do not. Add exact assertions to the kit's tests: comparator calls (T-15), `valueToJSON` calls (T-21), timers (T-26, T-27, T-28), listeners (T-07), popup roots (T-08), renders per patch (T-06). They are cheap, run in CI on every PR, and would have caught each regression class in the register.
3. **G-T3: WKWebView.** Several decisions here were taken in Chromium. Two native-only symptoms are on record: "native black rows" during fast scroll (not reproduced by the fixture, `bench/app/README.md:544`; lib #126's limit) and timer calls that are "slow in WKWebView" (host #487 and lib #126, the reason for T-27 and T-28). Plan 1a.11 is the only place they can show up; add "fast scroll shows no blank rows" and "timer calls during an 8 s scroll" to its checklist.
4. **G-T4: metric states.** The `bench/app` cells `--metrics hidden,unavailable,healthy` (`bench/app/README.md:400`) exist for the old table only. The new page needs the same three states in its real-page test (hidden: 0 observations; unavailable: static gaps, 0 pulsing; healthy), because the largest recorded cost among the retained choices (healthy target slots +21.1%) sits there.
5. **G-T5: memory.** Recorded once: retained JS heap after three scroll cycles and a full GC 74.81 to 73.58 MiB (host #470 comment), and "Not verified: memory held by four lingering views on a very large cluster" (host #487). No guard exists for retained DOM or views after open, scroll and close cycles. Add a leak check to 1a.3's bench: after ten open, scroll, close cycles the heap and the number of open source views return to the start (the host #470 comment reports "Closing the tab left zero source views and zero active metric observations" as a manual check).

One mismatch for the lead: **B2's budget is stricter than anything recorded for the old table.** The old table's best unthrottled no-update result is rAF p95 33.4 ms and it "still miss[es] the 20 ms frame goal" (`bench/app/README.md:942`); with sparse updates the candidate recorded 66.7 ms (`bench/app/README.md:945`). B2 asks for 16.7 ms. That is a fine target for a rewrite, but a miss against B2 is not by itself a regression against today's product, and trigger T1 should say which of the two it measures.

## 2. Resource data layer

Slice 1a.2 (plan decision D3) reads the data chain through the runtime hooks unchanged: `useConnectionReadiness`, `useResourceTypes`, `useResolvedResourceTarget`, `useResources({ collection: true, live })`, with an explicit `pluginID`. Everything in 2.1 therefore stays where it is and the new tree gets it for free, **as long as it keeps calling the hooks the way the old table does** and adds none of the hazards named in the Verdict column. 2.2 to 2.6 are the parts below the hooks (host Go, SDK, the plugins), which decide what the hooks can assume: frame sizes, ordering, no-op filtering, queue bounds, retry policy.

Two facts to keep in mind from the start. First, the first row of a collection waits for the whole snapshot: a source view stages snapshot frames in a private map and publishes at `snapshotEnd` (`RT/resource/v2/sourceView.ts:194`, `:210-223`), which is plan item E10 and decision A10. Second, **the benchmark fixture and the real host disagree about frame size**: the fixture emits snapshot batches of 256 records (`bench/render/fixtures/kubernetes.ts:222`), while the host's default frame is 16 items (`defaultSourceMaxBatchItems = 16`, `host:backend/pkg/plugin/resource/source_coordinator.go:25`), so a real 5,000-Pod snapshot is about 313 frames, not 20. A fixture number says nothing about frame-count cost.

Go code stays. The new tree reaches it only through the runtime (`useResources`, `useSourceStates`, the metric and graph observation hooks) and must not call `ResourceClient` or `Call`. T-22, T-23, T-25 and T-27 are the runtime halves of rows below; these rows give the Go half and what the UI may and may not assume.

| ID | Where | What it does, and why | Evidence | Verdict | Guard | Slice |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

On managedFields-heavy Pods, 16 Pods plus encoder scratch use about 195 MB of the 256 MiB transient pool and 32 use about 260 MB. Each frame becomes one view event: a `changes` event holds at most 16 records. | host #383: "negotiate 16-record batches for managedFields-heavy Pods"; "measured headroom, not a guarantee for arbitrary frames". Comment at the cited line: about 195 MB at 16 Pods, about 260 MB at 32. No latency number. | CARRY AS ENGINE, **MNR**: stays; the new tree consumes `useResources` and must not assume large `changes` batches. Rule: handle at most 16 records per `changes` event and many events per second, behind the runtime's 100 ms delivery (T-23); never size anything from a UI-side batch constant. | G-existing: `host:backend/pkg/plugin/resource/source_coordinator_test.go:121` asserts 16 in the open request; `:139` rejects a 17th change. ### 2.4 Kubernetes plugin, Go side

All rows here are Go code that stays. Their UI constraint (what the new tree must not assume) is in the Verdict column. The UI-side halves are T-21 to T-27; no Go finding repeats a T row, so none is mapped.

| ID | Where | What it does, and why | Evidence | Verdict | Guard | Slice |

| K8G-1, K8G-2 | `k8s:pkg/plugin/resource/resourcers/base.go:241-253`, `k8s:pkg/plugin/resource/clients/clients.go:23` | The source handler drops an informer update whose resourceVersion, UID, name, namespace, apiVersion and kind equal the old object's, so it is never converted, encoded or sent. ## 3. Log viewer

The log viewer is the host's: plugins open sessions through the runtime (`useLogs`) and render nothing. It is a virtualised list over one mutable, capped buffer that renders once per frame, with ingest, ANSI and search work done once per line or once per appended line. The rows in 3.1 are the register; 3.2 and 3.3 are the plugin and Go sides that decide what the viewer may assume (batch sizes, what a pause drops, how early output is kept). Defects found while reading are labelled "Defect, do not carry" in the Verdict column: the unbounded pause buffer (HLT-6) and, in the terminal, stdout and stderr listeners that are never removed on teardown (HLT-28, in 8.1). The table techniques that apply to a log list (reference-equality row memo, static classes, measured-row windowing) are T-06, T-02 and T-05.


### 3.1 Log viewer (host)

The viewer is `LogViewerContainer` with its hooks and utils under `host:ui/providers/BottomDrawer/containers/LogViewer/`. A kit log list may reuse from the table register: T-05 (the virtual snapshot adapter, with measured heights), T-06 and T-32 (row memo on the original object, stable inputs), T-09 (rows read no shell context), T-12 (hover in CSS) and T-10 (a stable mutable array is read stale under the compiler unless a version is a dependency). T-02, T-03 and T-04 are for fixed-height rows and apply to a log list only with wrap off. Log-specific, in no T row: the 100,000-entry cap with head eviction, the one-flush-per-frame buffer, the pinned-bottom logic, the search bounds and ANSI and level parsing at ingest. Go delivery of lines is HGO-31 and HGO-32 (section 2.2); plugin readers are section 3.2.

### 3.2 Logs in the plugins

#### Kubernetes plugin Go

Go side of pod logs: finite bounds, streaming clients, source watching. The log viewer itself is in 3.1.

Follow streams have no tail or byte cap. | Unmeasured. `k8s:README.md:413`: "default to 200 tail lines, clamp at 5,000, and cap output at 1 MiB". | CARRY AS ENGINE **MNR**. Stays. The log viewer must not assume more than 5,000 lines or 1 MiB per finite read ("load more" pages by time) and must bound its own buffer for follow, which the plugin leaves unbounded. | G-existing: `k8s:pkg/plugin/logs/native_test.go:41` (finite bounds and options). ## 4. Terminal and bottom drawer

The host terminal is xterm.js with the WebGL addon and xterm's own DOM renderer as the fallback after a context loss; there is no canvas addon. Every drawer tab stays mounted and is hidden with `visibility`, not `display: none`, so a hidden terminal keeps its measured size (HSH-29). A session lives in the Go host: closing a tab, moving it to another window, or reattaching reuses the session and replays a bounded history once. Rows: 4.1 the host front end, 4.2 the plugins' Go shells, 4.3 the library's own terminal component (which the host does not use for its drawer), 4.4 the host's Go side.


### 4.1 Terminal and bottom drawer (host)

Rows for the host's `Terminal.tsx` and the drawer around it. The library's own terminal component is section 4.3 (LIB-20, LIB-21): the host does not use it, and the same rules apply to whichever wrapper the new panel uses. Backend halves are HGO-34, HGO-35 and HGO-36 (section 2.2). Terminal teardown and its listener defect are HLT-28 in section 8.1. Rows 32 to 40 are the drawer; HSH-29 and HSH-30 in the shell scout's rows describe the same code.

## 5. Editors

**There is no lazy Monaco today.** `ui/main.tsx:3` imports the Monaco bootstrap statically, so `monaco-editor` is 22.8% of the generated characters of the minified entry chunk (3,314,433 characters, `docs/ui-next/baseline/2026-11-16.md:86`); the library dropped its own lazy loading as ineffective (its changelog: "remove ineffective editor lazy loading"); lazy loading of the editor, terminal, routes and icons is planned (host issue #473), not done. What exists and must survive is the machinery around the engine: workers created per language on first use, one schema registry that flushes once per tick, schemas requested only by a mounted editor, one model per URI with an ordered disposal, and a few options that keep per-keystroke cost down. Decision C2 (Monaco now, CodeMirror 6 possibly) decides which of these carry code and which carry only the rule.


### 5.1 Editors: host

Monaco itself is loaded eagerly at startup (HSH-2, in 6.1). These rows cover how it is configured and how schemas reach it. Decision C2 (trimmed Monaco now, CodeMirror 6 possibly later) changes the engine, not the three rules below.

| ID | Where | What it does, and why | Evidence | Verdict | Guard | Slice |

Workers are separate Vite files (`?worker&url`, no blob), created by `MonacoEnvironment.getWorker` on first use. `loader.config({ monaco })` makes `@monaco-editor/react` use the bundled copy, so there is no CDN fetch. monaco-yaml is configured at module evaluation with `enableSchemaRequest: false`; a global flag tells the shared editors package to skip its own setup | Worker sizes, `host:docs/ui-next/baseline/2026-11-16.md:79`: ts.worker 12,093,286 B and 8,020,240 B, css.worker 1,829,870 B, yaml.worker.bundle 1,827,165 B. Host #198: "Monaco remains 0.52.2 after newer YAML-worker protocol failures". Rule: 0 CDN requests, 0 blob workers, 0 workers at app start | G-existing: none (`host:vite.config.ts:154` swaps the module for `bootstrap.mock.ts` under vitest). ## 6. Startup, plugin loading and build

The entry chunk is one file holding the host and Monaco: 23,045,947 B unminified, 14,554,523 B minified (host #508); there is no `manualChunks`, no `modulePreload`, no `React.lazy` in `ui/`, and every page is a static import. The shell renders before plugins finish; plugin UIs load in bounded parallel chunks after first render. `OMNIVIEW_UI=next` today builds a 229,936 B entry (minified). The rows keep what makes startup cheap or safe (minify, shared singletons, bounded plugin loading, the first-paint theme script, the boot chain that releases the window restore) and flag what is a cost the new tree must not inherit (Monaco and two whole icon sets in the first-screen chunk).

## 7. Tabs, panes, windows and the panel: keep-alive and unmount policy

Today's policy, read from the code: **a tab renders only after it has been shown once, then stays mounted in a hidden `<Activity>` until it closes**, with no cap (HSH-21). React destroys the effects of a hidden tree, so hidden pages stop their timers and subscriptions (**[INFERENCE]**: no measurement exists for N hidden tabs). Drawer tabs are different: every one stays mounted, hidden with `visibility` (HSH-29). Source views of hidden or closed pages linger 60 s, at most 4 (RTM-05), so a page that is unmounted releases its collection to that policy rather than to a tab-level cache. The new shell's first slice has to decide this policy on purpose; the rows below are what the old one guarantees and which of it is tested (almost none).


### 7.1 Tabs, panes and windows

Hidden tab pages stay mounted: a tab shown at least once stays in a hidden Activity until it closes or leaves the window; tabs never shown are not mounted. T-25 (view linger, 60 s and 4 views) and T-26 (age timer) are the data and timer sides of the same policy. Drawer rows are here because the tracker groups panel keep-alive with tabs; their content is in 4.1.

| ID | Where | What it does, and why | Evidence | Verdict | Guard | Slice |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Shown tabs stay mounted until closed or moved away: `<Activity mode='hidden'>` when inactive (React destroys their effects and defers their renders) and `display: none` on the host div. Hosts are moved between panes with `appendChild`, so split and move never remount. No cap | Host #199: "Every tab is a route with its own memory router and stays mounted while hidden (`<Activity>`)". No number for N hidden tabs. [INFERENCE] hidden react-query observers unsubscribe, so their intervals stop | RE-EXPRESS, **MNR**: tab state survives switches without keeping hidden effects alive, and unshown tabs are not mounted. Rules: restoring N tabs mounts 1 page per pane; hidden pages hold 0 live intervals or subscriptions at 10 hidden tabs; moving a tab between panes remounts 0 pages | G-existing: none (no test imports `TabPages`). ## 8. Events, subscriptions and idle timers

Events reach the frontend through a few module-level bridges and one event bus with per-key handler lists; Wails events are broadcast to every window and each consumer filters by payload (HSH-32), except metric and graph observations, which go to one window (HGO-18). The idle-timer table in 8.1 is the closed list of timers that run in a workspace window with nothing open; **any new always-on timer in the new shell is a regression unless it is gated by the page-visible signal** (HSH-22) and added to that list.


### 8.1 Events, subscriptions and idle timers

#### Host log viewer, terminal and drawer

Log and terminal event plumbing. Listener and subscription pairing is where the old code has a real defect (HLT-28); the other three rows are bounds that stay.

## 9. First-party plugin UIs

Slice 1a leaves the Kubernetes plugin UI untouched; these rows say what each later port or rebuild must keep. The table techniques are in §1 (T-nn); the rows here are plugin-specific. Flux and Proxmox are ported before the flip by their owners; AWS and Containers are rebuilt after it (R19). Of the four plugins other than Kubernetes only AWS has a virtualised table (fixed 30 px rows); Containers and Flux mount every row, and Proxmox pages client-side at 256 rows with no virtualiser. None of those four has a render-count or mounted-row test.


### 9.1 Kubernetes plugin UI beyond the Pods table

Dashboard, events list, home cards and sidebar. None of this is in slice 1a; the rows say what the later port must keep. The shared age timer and metric demand are T-26 and T-27.

## 10. Guards that exist today, and what they cannot see

Two questions per guard: does it run in CI (and would it fail a pull request), and would it notice a regression in the new tree. The answers are mostly "no" and "no": **no benchmark in the repository can fail a pull request, and none of them mounts `ui-next/` or `packages/kit`.** What does fail a pull request is functional: unit and component tests, the runtime's own tests, the age-clock DST test, the compiler admission test, the flavour guard.

### 10.0 The host's benchmark and guard infrastructure

Reference numbers: `docs/ui-next/baseline/2026-11-16.md:126-145` | Yes, on every pull request (`.github/workflows/render-bench.yml:71`), **report only**: it uploads `render-bench-results` and a job summary and has no threshold. It imports the plugin UI from the latest published release tag (`bench/render/README.md:18`) | No. It mounts the old shell's providers and the old plugin table. ### 10.1 Guards in the plugins, the library and the host (existing benchmarks and exact-count tests)

#### Host log viewer, terminal and drawer

Host `ui/` tests run in CI through `pnpm test` (`host:vite.config.ts:58`, `host:.github/workflows/pr-checks.yml:312`); they live in the old UI tree, so each is a port candidate at the flip. Runtime tests run in the runtime step (`host:.github/workflows/pr-checks.yml:328`); Go tests through `task check:go-test` (`host:.github/workflows/pr-checks.yml:133`). The two bench jobs report numbers and gate on nothing but errors (`host:.github/workflows/render-bench.yml:71`, `host:.github/workflows/app-profile.yml:147`); e2e runs through the canonical runner on pull requests with application changes (`host:.github/workflows/pr.yml:23`). No Profiler or render-count assertion exists in the viewer, terminal or drawer tests, and no benchmark has a terminal.

#### The library

Library CI at the pin: `lib:.github/workflows/codspeed.yml:25-75` runs the benchmarks under CodSpeed CPU simulation in 4 shards on `ubuntu-24.04`; `lib:.github/workflows/pr-storybook-preview.yml:61` runs `pnpm test` (the component tests) on pull requests. No benchmark references `DataTable.VirtualBody`, `MetricCell`, `CapacityBar`, `TooltipScope`, an editor, the terminal or `ai-ui`; CodSpeed reported lib #126 as "not alter performance". All of it is frozen at the flip: every row below stops protecting the new tree and is a port candidate.

## 11. The MUST NOT REGRESS list

One entry per requirement, grouped by area. "Rows" names the register rows that carry the evidence; "Guard" says what protects it today (`G-existing`) or the exact assertion to add (`G-new`); "Slice" is the plan step or later slice that touches it. A slice that touches an entry keeps it, re-expresses it with the same count or time, or records a measured decision against it. The 16 table entries are the ones of 1.5, repeated in one line each so this list stands alone.

### 11.1 Tables (details and guards in 1.5)

| ID | Must hold | Rows | Slice |
|---|---|---|---|
| MNR-T1 | At most 3,000 mounted nodes at rest and 40 elements per row on 5,000 rows | T-01, T-34 | 1a.3, 1a.7 |
| MNR-T2 | No coverage hole when scrolling 5,000 rows; the mounted range moves in 4-row steps unless measured otherwise | T-02, T-05 | 1a.3, 1a.7 |
| MNR-T3 | Row layout (absolute rows or spacers) chosen by measurement under live updates | T-03 | 1a.3 |
| MNR-T4 | A record patch renders 1 row; a selection click at most 2 rows in 1 commit; an unrelated parent or shell update 0; hover 0 commits | T-06, T-09, T-12 | 1a.3, 1a.7, 1a.8 |
| MNR-T5 | One delegated click handler; listener count equal at 60 and 5,000 rows | T-07 | 1a.3, 1a.7 |
| MNR-T6 | One popup owner per table; at most 10 tooltip roots at 5,000 rows; hover-only controls not mounted per row | T-08, T-31 | 1a.4d, 1a.3, 1a.8 |
| MNR-T7 | 50 two-row patches over 5,000 rows make at most 300,000 comparator calls | T-15 | 1a.3, 1a.7 |
| MNR-T8 | One decode per `HostRecord` object; one collection observer per page; table inputs keep their identity | T-21, T-22, T-32 | 1a.2, 1a.7 |
| MNR-T9 | One timer for all Age cells; none while hidden; cadence follows the mock's label rule | T-26 | 1a.7 |
| MNR-T10 | Hidden columns never mount; hidden metric columns open no observation | T-17 | 1a.7, 1a.9, 1a.10 |
| MNR-T11 | Metric demand: register in an effect, one timer per burst, no per-cell subscription, one shared pulse timer, no size container on a placeholder | T-27, T-28, T-33 | 1a.10 |
| MNR-T12 | Selection pruning only with a non-empty selection and a complete snapshot | T-18 | 1a.8 |
| MNR-T13 | Search commits once per 500 ms burst | T-19 | 1a.6 |
| MNR-T14 | No infinite animation at rest | T-29 | 1a.7, 1a.8 |
| MNR-T15 | Pinned columns stay sticky in fixed rows; focus survives a row leaving the window | T-11, T-13 | 1a.3 |
| MNR-T16 | Rows never read shell contexts; the table consumes `useResources` and nothing below it | T-09, T-23..T-25 | 1a.2, 1a.7 |

## 12. What I could not assess

Read-only, one session, no benchmark run. Everything below is either unmeasured in the sources or beyond what reading could settle.

**Cross-cutting**

- **No number in this file is mine.** I ran no benchmark, no profile and no test. Every figure is quoted from a pull request, a benchmark README, a doc or a recorded evidence file, with its source beside it. The only evidence file I did not open is `bench/app/renderer-sprint-metal-evidence-2026-11-10.json` (the numbers come from the pull request comments that cite it).
- **WKWebView.** Almost every recorded number is Chromium with an in-page fake backend on an M5 Max, mostly with software rendering or short diagnostic protocols. The native app was used for two sets of numbers only (host #487, Kubernetes #257). Whether the old optimisations help, or the rejected experiments hurt, in WKWebView, on Windows (WebView2) or on Linux is unknown. The N8 baseline says the same about itself (`docs/ui-next/baseline/2026-11-16.md:147-153`).
- **Idle CPU and GPU, memory outside the JS heap, parse and compile time.** The reported 53.5% Graphics and 35.6% WebContent CPU on a 300-Pod cluster was never attributed (`docs/kubernetes-performance-investigation-2026-10-29.md:5`). The cost of N kept-alive hidden tabs, of a hidden drawer tab that keeps ingesting, and of a 14.6 MB entry chunk in WKWebView has no measurement on `main`.
- **Real-cluster and IPC behaviour.** Fixture numbers exclude the Wails bridge, gRPC, the plugin process and the API server. The only real-cluster figures are the plan's own (1,025 and 5,025 real Pods: first row 0.7 s and 2.7 s, `docs/ui-next/slices/1a-pods-list.md:222`).
- **Row and object counts reachable per plugin table.** Cluster and account sizes are not in the code, so every "can mount N rows" claim for Containers, AWS, Flux and Proxmox tables is unmeasured.
- **Third-party plugins** (decision C5) and the marketplace web UI are out of scope.
- **Inferences from greps, not runs.** Where a row says a library component has "no consumer in the host or a first-party plugin", it rests on a search of the pinned trees, not on a call graph. Rows say `[INFERENCE]` where it matters.
- **Wails internals.** Event routing to windows and the single mailbox were read from Wails v3.1.0 in the Go module cache, outside the pinned trees (rows say `[INFERENCE]`).
- **The Containers remote.** The canonical clone's `origin` is `joshuapare/omniview-plugin-containers`, an owner fork; an organisation repository of the same plugin, if one exists, may differ.
- **Open work.** Branches and pull requests that are not on `main` were read for their numbers but their code was not assessed (Appendix A).

**Per area**

- Tables: the cost of each of the plugin's other 47 `Table.tsx` kinds (only their use of the data-only opt-in was counted); how many users resize columns; whether a native `<table>` with fixed layout and spacer rows is cheaper than the old absolutely positioned rows (the one question 1a.3 must answer, T-03).
- Data layer: the effect of the 16-item host frame against the plugin's 256-record batch on a real cluster (no number found); the size of the snapshot a user waits for at 20,000 Pods beyond plan E10's two points.
- Go side: no Go `Benchmark*` was run, and none runs in CI; the host's `BenchmarkHostSourceSnapshot20KPods` and the SDK's open performance pull request (sdk #58) were not run.
- Editors: the live cost of Monaco and its workers in the entry chunk (bytes are exact, parse time is not); whether the new editor engine is Monaco or CodeMirror 6 (decision C2) decides which rows carry code.
- Startup: the Go-readiness retries are listed (ruling R13), but whether each is still needed after the channel changes was not tested.
